I came because I just... I can’t get this feeling out of my head that I’m being punished. Like some kind of judgment has already been made against me because of how my father declined, and I should have done something different.

She’s always been the organized one, you know, folders and tabs and copies of everything. But even when I look at it, it doesn’t change anything inside. It still feels like I’m guilty somehow.

Yeah… I think maybe more slow than restless.

People at work have started asking if I’m sick, which is embarrassing because I don’t feel like I’m doing anything different on purpose. I’ll just be trying to get through something routine, like opening a file or answering an email, and it’s like my body is moving through mud. It reminds me of when the copier jams and everyone stands there pressing the same button even though it clearly isn’t going to work. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes, I guess people have noticed I’m slower. I don’t think I’ve been pacing around or anything like that.

But yes… roughly fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really think about pleasure the way I used to, I guess.\n\nLike, I used to have routines, things I did after work or on weekends, but now when someone asks me what I enjoy, my mind kind of goes blank. It reminds me of when my coworker asked if I was taking any vacation time, and I started explaining our PTO rollover policy instead of answering her, which was… not what she meant.\n\nSo, I don’t know. I can’t really give you a clean answer. I haven’t been paying attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking it.\n\nI mean, my clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I think maybe they were just washed wrong or I’m wearing the older black pants from work, the ones with the waistband that never sat right even before all this. I used to keep receipts for returns in this little envelope in my desk, and now half the time I can’t even find the envelope, so weighing myself regularly has not really been something I’ve kept up with.\n\nSo… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a significant change. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I… not like a plan. I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself thinking, some mornings, that it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Especially when I already know there are going to be calls from the care agency, and then work problems waiting, and I’m trying to remember which voicemail was from who… It’s not that I’m setting out to do something. It’s more like… not wanting to have to start the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless and unable to sit still since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe more slow than restless.\n\nPeople at work have started asking if I’m sick, which is embarrassing because I don’t feel like I’m doing anything different on purpose. I’ll just be trying to get through something routine, like opening a file or answering an email, and it’s like my body is moving through mud. And in meetings, someone will ask me something simple, something I should be able to answer right away, and there’s this… pause. Like I hear them, but it takes me a second to make myself respond.\n\nIt reminds me of when the copier jams and everyone stands there pressing the same button even though it clearly isn’t going to work. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes, I guess people have noticed I’m slower. Yeah… I guess I have. Not always in the obvious way, like telling people “I’m not coming.” It’s more that I stop answering, or I let things slide until it’s too late to go.

I don’t really think about pleasure the way I used to, I guess.\n\nLike, I used to have routines, things I did after work or on weekends, but now when someone asks me what I enjoy, my mind kind of goes blank. It reminds me of when my coworker asked if I was taking any vacation time, and I started explaining our PTO rollover policy instead of answering her, which was… not what she meant.\n\nSo, I don’t know. I can’t really give you a clean answer.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF43.1, F32.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came because I just... I can’t get this feeling out of my head that I’m being punished. Like some kind of judgment has already been made against me because of how my father declined, and I should have done something different.\n\nMy sister keeps showing me things, doctor’s notes and all that, saying I did everything right, that there wasn’t more I could’ve done. She’s always been the organized one, you know, folders and tabs and copies of everything. But even when I look at it, it doesn’t change anything inside. It still feels like I’m guilty somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling, like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nI’ll be sitting at my desk in HR, looking at forms or emails, and it’s like none of it can really get through to me. Or I’ll stare at the garden beds at home — I used to notice what needed trimming, what was coming up — and now it just feels far away, like it doesn’t reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about something specific that you did or didn't do regarding your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not one single thing, exactly. I keep replaying all of it — every appointment I scheduled or didn’t schedule fast enough, every phone call, every time I got short with my relatives because they were asking the same questions over and over.\n\nAnd then I’ll be at work, trying to answer some benefits question, and I think, how can I even help anyone when I couldn’t help my own father? It makes me feel like I’m selfish… like I was a bad daughter. And then I feel useless at work too, like I deserve whatever comes next.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your father declined?\n\nPatient: About… a year and two months, I think. It started when my father’s health and behavior began changing and I had to step in more, with caregiving and decisions and all of that.\n\nIt’s strange, because I can remember little details from then — like the folder I started keeping papers in, and my sister telling me I needed better dividers for it — but the time itself feels blurry. But yes… roughly fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really think about pleasure the way I used to, I guess.\n\nLike, I used to have routines, things I did after work or on weekends, but now when someone asks me what I enjoy, my mind kind of goes blank. It reminds me of when my coworker asked if I was taking any vacation time, and I started explaining our PTO rollover policy instead of answering her, which was… not what she meant.\n\nSo, I don’t know. I can’t really give you a clean answer. I haven’t been paying attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking it.\n\nI mean, my clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I think maybe they were just washed wrong or I’m wearing the older black pants from work, the ones with the waistband that never sat right even before all this. I used to keep receipts for returns in this little envelope in my desk, and now half the time I can’t even find the envelope, so weighing myself regularly has not really been something I’ve kept up with.\n\nSo… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a significant change. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I… not like a plan. I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself thinking, some mornings, that it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Especially when I already know there are going to be calls from the care agency, and then work problems waiting, and I’m trying to remember which voicemail was from who… It’s not that I’m setting out to do something. It’s more like… not wanting to have to start the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless and unable to sit still since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe more slow than restless.\n\nPeople at work have started asking if I’m sick, which is embarrassing because I don’t feel like I’m doing anything different on purpose. I’ll just be trying to get through something routine, like opening a file or answering an email, and it’s like my body is moving through mud. And in meetings, someone will ask me something simple, something I should be able to answer right away, and there’s this… pause. Like I hear them, but it takes me a second to make myself respond.\n\nIt reminds me of when the copier jams and everyone stands there pressing the same button even though it clearly isn’t going to work. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes, I guess people have noticed I’m slower. I don’t think I’ve been pacing around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending time with friends and family since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Not always in the obvious way, like telling people “I’m not coming.” It’s more that I stop answering, or I let things slide until it’s too late to go.\n\nAt work, I’ve missed deadlines I normally would’ve been on top of, and I’ve avoided some difficult employee meetings—ones I used to handle all the time. I keep thinking I’ll get myself together enough to do it, and then the day just… moves past me.\n\nAnd the library volunteering, I haven’t been showing up for that. I used to like shelving returns because it was quiet and there was this one older man who always asked for mystery books but never remembered the authors’ names—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I just… I haven’t been going because I can’t keep myself steady enough to follow through. So yes, I think I’ve been pulling away.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline that you still find yourself looking forward to or participating in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of anything I really look forward to in the way I used to.\n\nI still do some things, I guess, but it feels more like because they’re on the calendar or because someone expects me to. Like I’ll have the thought, “Oh, I used to like that,” but the actual pull toward it isn’t really there. Even little things—stopping for coffee from that place near work, or browsing books at the library—somehow it all feels like too much effort, and then I end up just going home.\n\nSorry, I know I’m sort of circling around it. I think the answer is… no, not much comes to mind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I… I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of one, and my mind kind of jumps around.

Like, I used to be the person who had little routines—coffee, books, maybe texting someone back because something funny happened at work. There was this mug in the break room with a chip in it that everyone hated using, and for some reason I always thought it was funny when it ended up on my desk… Sorry, that’s not really an answer.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit.

I keep replaying every decision I made about my dad’s appointments—whether I pushed hard enough, whether I missed something, whether I was too tired that day and it showed. And then I start thinking about the conversations with my relatives, the tense ones, where everyone had an opinion and I’d get defensive or shut down.

It all sort of piles up, and then it turns into this thought that I’m just… a selfish daughter. Like I didn’t do it right, or I didn’t care enough, even though I know I was trying. And then it spreads to work too, like I’m useless there, too, because I can’t keep up the way I used to.

Sometimes it feels like whatever bad thing happens, I probably deserve it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it doesn’t stay neatly about my dad, if that’s what you mean.

It starts there, usually—like I’ll go over every appointment I took him to, every call I made or didn’t make, and then those arguments or tense talks with relatives, where I think maybe I was too sharp or too defensive. But then it spreads. I’ll be at work and make some small mistake, or even just not answer something fast enough, and suddenly it feels like proof that I’m useless there too.

I don’t know if that counts as unrelated, because it all kind of connects in my head. Like I’m a selfish daughter, and then a bad employee, and then just… someone who deserves whatever happens.

I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself thinking, some mornings, that it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Especially when I already know there are going to be calls from the care agency, and then work problems waiting, and I’m trying to remember which voicemail was from who… It’s not that I’m setting out to do something. It’s more like… not wanting to have to start the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless and unable to sit still since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe more slow than restless.\n\nPeople at work have started asking if I’m sick, which is embarrassing because I don’t feel like I’m doing anything different on purpose. I’ll just be trying to get through something routine, like opening a file or answering an email, and it’s like my body is moving through mud. And in meetings, someone will ask me something simple, something I should be able to answer right away, and there’s this… pause. Like I hear them, but it takes me a second to make myself respond.\n\nIt reminds me of when the copier jams and everyone stands there pressing the same button even though it clearly isn’t going to work. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes, I guess people have noticed I’m slower. I don’t think I’ve been pacing around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending time with friends and family since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Not always in the obvious way, like telling people “I’m not coming.” It’s more that I stop answering, or I let things slide until it’s too late to go.\n\nAt work, I’ve missed deadlines I normally would’ve been on top of, and I’ve avoided some difficult employee meetings—ones I used to handle all the time. I keep thinking I’ll get myself together enough to do it, and then the day just… moves past me.\n\nAnd the library volunteering, I haven’t been showing up for that. I used to like shelving returns because it was quiet and there was this one older man who always asked for mystery books but never remembered the authors’ names—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I just… I haven’t been going because I can’t keep myself steady enough to follow through. So yes, I think I’ve been pulling away.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline that you still find yourself looking forward to or participating in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of anything I really look forward to in the way I used to.\n\nI still do some things, I guess, but it feels more like because they’re on the calendar or because someone expects me to. Like I’ll have the thought, “Oh, I used to like that,” but the actual pull toward it isn’t really there. Even little things—stopping for coffee from that place near work, or browsing books at the library—somehow it all feels like too much effort, and then I end up just going home.\n\nSorry, I know I’m sort of circling around it. I think the answer is… no, not much comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find joy or interest in?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of one, and my mind kind of jumps around.\n\nLike, I used to be the person who had little routines—coffee, books, maybe texting someone back because something funny happened at work. There was this mug in the break room with a chip in it that everyone hated using, and for some reason I always thought it was funny when it ended up on my desk… Sorry, that’s not really an answer.\n\nI guess I don’t know if I’d call it joy. Sometimes something is distracting for a minute, or it’s familiar, but I don’t really feel that interest grabbing me the way it used to. I’m having a hard time naming anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're a bad person because of what happened with your father?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit.\n\nI keep replaying every decision I made about my dad’s appointments—whether I pushed hard enough, whether I missed something, whether I was too tired that day and it showed. And then I start thinking about the conversations with my relatives, the tense ones, where everyone had an opinion and I’d get defensive or shut down.\n\nIt all sort of piles up, and then it turns into this thought that I’m just… a selfish daughter. Like I didn’t do it right, or I didn’t care enough, even though I know I was trying. And then it spreads to work too, like I’m useless there, too, because I can’t keep up the way I used to.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad thing happens, I probably deserve it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things unrelated to your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t stay neatly about my dad, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt starts there, usually—like I’ll go over every appointment I took him to, every call I made or didn’t make, and then those arguments or tense talks with relatives, where I think maybe I was too sharp or too defensive. But then it spreads. I’ll be at work and make some small mistake, or even just not answer something fast enough, and suddenly it feels like proof that I’m useless there too.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as unrelated, because it all kind of connects in my head. Like I’m a selfish daughter, and then a bad employee, and then just… someone who deserves whatever happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I think so. I mean, I know I keep circling back to my dad, but it doesn’t stop there.

Like at work, if I forget to follow up on something or I don’t answer an email quickly enough, I’ll think about it all day. And it turns into this bigger thing, like, “Of course you messed that up, you’re useless.” Even little daily things start feeling like evidence against me.

And then my mind goes back to the appointments, the tense conversations with relatives, all of it… and I end up feeling like I was a selfish daughter and I’m a useless employee, and maybe I deserve whatever happens. It’s hard to separate it out.
=====================================

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came because I just... I can’t get this feeling out of my head that I’m being punished. Like some kind of judgment has already been made against me because of how my father declined, and I should have done something different.\n\nMy sister keeps showing me things, doctor’s notes and all that, saying I did everything right, that there wasn’t more I could’ve done. She’s always been the organized one, you know, folders and tabs and copies of everything. But even when I look at it, it doesn’t change anything inside. It still feels like I’m guilty somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling, like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nI’ll be sitting at my desk in HR, looking at forms or emails, and it’s like none of it can really get through to me. Or I’ll stare at the garden beds at home — I used to notice what needed trimming, what was coming up — and now it just feels far away, like it doesn’t reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about something specific that you did or didn't do regarding your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not one single thing, exactly. I keep replaying all of it — every appointment I scheduled or didn’t schedule fast enough, every phone call, every time I got short with my relatives because they were asking the same questions over and over.\n\nAnd then I’ll be at work, trying to answer some benefits question, and I think, how can I even help anyone when I couldn’t help my own father? It makes me feel like I’m selfish… like I was a bad daughter. And then I feel useless at work too, like I deserve whatever comes next.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your father declined?\n\nPatient: About… a year and two months, I think. It started when my father’s health and behavior began changing and I had to step in more, with caregiving and decisions and all of that.\n\nIt’s strange, because I can remember little details from then — like the folder I started keeping papers in, and my sister telling me I needed better dividers for it — but the time itself feels blurry. But yes… roughly fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really think about pleasure the way I used to, I guess.\n\nLike, I used to have routines, things I did after work or on weekends, but now when someone asks me what I enjoy, my mind kind of goes blank. It reminds me of when my coworker asked if I was taking any vacation time, and I started explaining our PTO rollover policy instead of answering her, which was… not what she meant.\n\nSo, I don’t know. I can’t really give you a clean answer. I haven’t been paying attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking it.\n\nI mean, my clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I think maybe they were just washed wrong or I’m wearing the older black pants from work, the ones with the waistband that never sat right even before all this. I used to keep receipts for returns in this little envelope in my desk, and now half the time I can’t even find the envelope, so weighing myself regularly has not really been something I’ve kept up with.\n\nSo… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a significant change. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I… not like a plan. I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself thinking, some mornings, that it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Especially when I already know there are going to be calls from the care agency, and then work problems waiting, and I’m trying to remember which voicemail was from who… It’s not that I’m setting out to do something. It’s more like… not wanting to have to start the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless and unable to sit still since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe more slow than restless.\n\nPeople at work have started asking if I’m sick, which is embarrassing because I don’t feel like I’m doing anything different on purpose. I’ll just be trying to get through something routine, like opening a file or answering an email, and it’s like my body is moving through mud. And in meetings, someone will ask me something simple, something I should be able to answer right away, and there’s this… pause. Like I hear them, but it takes me a second to make myself respond.\n\nIt reminds me of when the copier jams and everyone stands there pressing the same button even though it clearly isn’t going to work. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes, I guess people have noticed I’m slower. I don’t think I’ve been pacing around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending time with friends and family since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Not always in the obvious way, like telling people “I’m not coming.” It’s more that I stop answering, or I let things slide until it’s too late to go.\n\nAt work, I’ve missed deadlines I normally would’ve been on top of, and I’ve avoided some difficult employee meetings—ones I used to handle all the time. I keep thinking I’ll get myself together enough to do it, and then the day just… moves past me.\n\nAnd the library volunteering, I haven’t been showing up for that. I used to like shelving returns because it was quiet and there was this one older man who always asked for mystery books but never remembered the authors’ names—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I just… I haven’t been going because I can’t keep myself steady enough to follow through. So yes, I think I’ve been pulling away.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline that you still find yourself looking forward to or participating in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of anything I really look forward to in the way I used to.\n\nI still do some things, I guess, but it feels more like because they’re on the calendar or because someone expects me to. Like I’ll have the thought, “Oh, I used to like that,” but the actual pull toward it isn’t really there. Even little things—stopping for coffee from that place near work, or browsing books at the library—somehow it all feels like too much effort, and then I end up just going home.\n\nSorry, I know I’m sort of circling around it. I think the answer is… no, not much comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find joy or interest in?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of one, and my mind kind of jumps around.\n\nLike, I used to be the person who had little routines—coffee, books, maybe texting someone back because something funny happened at work. There was this mug in the break room with a chip in it that everyone hated using, and for some reason I always thought it was funny when it ended up on my desk… Sorry, that’s not really an answer.\n\nI guess I don’t know if I’d call it joy. Sometimes something is distracting for a minute, or it’s familiar, but I don’t really feel that interest grabbing me the way it used to. I’m having a hard time naming anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're a bad person because of what happened with your father?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit.\n\nI keep replaying every decision I made about my dad’s appointments—whether I pushed hard enough, whether I missed something, whether I was too tired that day and it showed. And then I start thinking about the conversations with my relatives, the tense ones, where everyone had an opinion and I’d get defensive or shut down.\n\nIt all sort of piles up, and then it turns into this thought that I’m just… a selfish daughter. Like I didn’t do it right, or I didn’t care enough, even though I know I was trying. And then it spreads to work too, like I’m useless there, too, because I can’t keep up the way I used to.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad thing happens, I probably deserve it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things unrelated to your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t stay neatly about my dad, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt starts there, usually—like I’ll go over every appointment I took him to, every call I made or didn’t make, and then those arguments or tense talks with relatives, where I think maybe I was too sharp or too defensive. But then it spreads. I’ll be at work and make some small mistake, or even just not answer something fast enough, and suddenly it feels like proof that I’m useless there too.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as unrelated, because it all kind of connects in my head. Like I’m a selfish daughter, and then a bad employee, and then just… someone who deserves whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things you did or didn't do in your daily life, unrelated to your father's health or decline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It makes me feel like I’m selfish… like I was a bad daughter. And then I feel useless at work too, like I deserve whatever comes next.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your father declined?\n\nPatient: About… a year and two months, I think. It started when my father’s health and behavior began changing and I had to step in more, with caregiving and decisions and all of that.\n\nIt’s strange, because I can remember little details from then — like the folder I started keeping papers in, and my sister telling me I needed better dividers for it — but the time itself feels blurry. But yes… roughly fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really think about pleasure the way I used to, I guess.\n\nLike, I used to have routines, things I did after work or on weekends, but now when someone asks me what I enjoy, my mind kind of goes blank. It reminds me of when my coworker asked if I was taking any vacation time, and I started explaining our PTO rollover policy instead of answering her, which was… not what she meant.\n\nSo, I don’t know. I can’t really give you a clean answer. I haven’t been paying attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking it.\n\nI mean, my clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I think maybe they were just washed wrong or I’m wearing the older black pants from work, the ones with the waistband that never sat right even before all this. I used to keep receipts for returns in this little envelope in my desk, and now half the time I can’t even find the envelope, so weighing myself regularly has not really been something I’ve kept up with.\n\nSo… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a significant change. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I… not like a plan. I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself thinking, some mornings, that it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Especially when I already know there are going to be calls from the care agency, and then work problems waiting, and I’m trying to remember which voicemail was from who… It’s not that I’m setting out to do something. It’s more like… not wanting to have to start the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless and unable to sit still since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe more slow than restless.\n\nPeople at work have started asking if I’m sick, which is embarrassing because I don’t feel like I’m doing anything different on purpose. I’ll just be trying to get through something routine, like opening a file or answering an email, and it’s like my body is moving through mud. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes, I guess people have noticed I’m slower. I don’t think I’ve been pacing around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending time with friends and family since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Not always in the obvious way, like telling people “I’m not coming.” It’s more that I stop answering, or I let things slide until it’s too late to go.\n\nAt work, I’ve missed deadlines I normally would’ve been on top of, and I’ve avoided some difficult employee meetings—ones I used to handle all the time. I keep thinking I’ll get myself together enough to do it, and then the day just… moves past me.\n\nAnd the library volunteering, I haven’t been showing up for that. I used to like shelving returns because it was quiet and there was this one older man who always asked for mystery books but never remembered the authors’ names—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I just… I haven’t been going because I can’t keep myself steady enough to follow through. judgment has already been passed on me.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: When the house is quiet at night, I sometimes hear a woman's voice as clearly as if she's in the hallway saying, 'You failed him,' and I've gotten up more than once to check even though no one is there.\n- key: S024\n  name: Depressed_Mood\n  description: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.\n  manifestation: Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the color has gone out of everything, and I can sit at my desk in HR or stare at the garden beds and feel like none of it reaches me anymore.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: I fall asleep late after replaying phone calls and care decisions, then I'm awake around 3 or 4 in the morning with my chest tight, unable to get back to sleep before work.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in simple questions in meetings.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep going over every decision about my father's appointments and every tense conversation with my relatives, and I end up feeling like I'm a selfish daughter and a useless employee who deserves whatever happens.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don't have a plan to hurt myself, but I catch myself thinking it would be easier if I didn't wake up, especially on mornings when I know there are more calls from the care agency and problems waiting at work.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n  manifestation: I never heard voices or believed I was being punished before this depression settled in around my father's decline; those experiences only started after months of feeling low, guilty, and worn down.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I've missed deadlines, avoided difficult employee meetings I normally handle, and stopped showing up for library volunteering because I can't keep myself steady enough to follow through.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and two months, since my father's health and behavior started changing and I had to step into caregiving.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 46 / human_resources_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things unrelated to your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t stay neatly about my dad, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt starts there, usually—like I’ll go over every appointment I took him to, every call I made or didn’t make, and then those arguments or tense talks with relatives, where I think maybe I was too sharp or too defensive. But then it spreads. I’ll be at work and make some small mistake, or even just not answer something fast enough, and suddenly it feels like proof that I’m useless there too.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as unrelated, because it all kind of connects in my head. Like I’m a selfish daughter, and then a bad employee, and then just… someone who deserves whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things you did or didn't do in your daily life, unrelated to your father's health or decline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. I mean, I know I keep circling back to my dad, but it doesn’t stop there.\n\nLike at work, if I forget to follow up on something or I don’t answer an email quickly enough, I’ll think about it all day. And it turns into this bigger thing, like, “Of course you messed that up, you’re useless.” Even little daily things start feeling like evidence against me.\n\nAnd then my mind goes back to the appointments, the tense conversations with relatives, all of it… and I end up feeling like I was a selfish daughter and I’m a useless employee, and maybe I deserve whatever happens. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling guilty about things unrelated to your father's health or decline?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about a year and a couple months. Maybe a year and two months, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt started around when my dad’s health and behavior started changing, and I had to step in more with caregiving. At first I thought it was just guilt about him, you know, but then it started attaching itself to other things too… work stuff, everyday things. I lose track of time a bit because that whole period feels like one long stretch, but… yeah, about a year and two months.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel guilty most of the time, even when you're not thinking about your father?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… most of the time, I think. Even when I’m not directly thinking about my father, it’s like the feeling is already there.

I’ll be doing something normal, like at work, and then I remember some decision I made about his appointments, or one of those tense conversations with relatives, and it all comes back around. But then it doesn’t stay there. It turns into… I’m selfish, I’m a bad daughter, and somehow also a useless employee, like everything I do proves it.

And I know that sounds dramatic. I used to tell employees at work not to talk to themselves that way, which is almost funny, except not really. But yes… it’s there a lot. Like I deserve whatever happens because I couldn’t get any of it right.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came because I just... I can’t get this feeling out of my head that I’m being punished. Like some kind of judgment has already been made against me because of how my father declined, and I should have done something different.\n\nMy sister keeps showing me things, doctor’s notes and all that, saying I did everything right, that there wasn’t more I could’ve done. She’s always been the organized one, you know, folders and tabs and copies of everything. But even when I look at it, it doesn’t change anything inside. It still feels like I’m guilty somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling, like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nI’ll be sitting at my desk in HR, looking at forms or emails, and it’s like none of it can really get through to me. Or I’ll stare at the garden beds at home — I used to notice what needed trimming, what was coming up — and now it just feels far away, like it doesn’t reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about something specific that you did or didn't do regarding your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not one single thing, exactly. I keep replaying all of it — every appointment I scheduled or didn’t schedule fast enough, every phone call, every time I got short with my relatives because they were asking the same questions over and over.\n\nAnd then I’ll be at work, trying to answer some benefits question, and I think, how can I even help anyone when I couldn’t help my own father? It makes me feel like I’m selfish… like I was a bad daughter. And then I feel useless at work too, like I deserve whatever comes next.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your father declined?\n\nPatient: About… a year and two months, I think. It started when my father’s health and behavior began changing and I had to step in more, with caregiving and decisions and all of that.\n\nIt’s strange, because I can remember little details from then — like the folder I started keeping papers in, and my sister telling me I needed better dividers for it — but the time itself feels blurry. But yes… roughly fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really think about pleasure the way I used to, I guess.\n\nLike, I used to have routines, things I did after work or on weekends, but now when someone asks me what I enjoy, my mind kind of goes blank. It reminds me of when my coworker asked if I was taking any vacation time, and I started explaining our PTO rollover policy instead of answering her, which was… not what she meant.\n\nSo, I don’t know. I can’t really give you a clean answer. I haven’t been paying attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking it.\n\nI mean, my clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I think maybe they were just washed wrong or I’m wearing the older black pants from work, the ones with the waistband that never sat right even before all this. I used to keep receipts for returns in this little envelope in my desk, and now half the time I can’t even find the envelope, so weighing myself regularly has not really been something I’ve kept up with.\n\nSo… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a significant change. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I… not like a plan. I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself thinking, some mornings, that it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Especially when I already know there are going to be calls from the care agency, and then work problems waiting, and I’m trying to remember which voicemail was from who… It’s not that I’m setting out to do something. It’s more like… not wanting to have to start the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless and unable to sit still since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe more slow than restless.\n\nPeople at work have started asking if I’m sick, which is embarrassing because I don’t feel like I’m doing anything different on purpose. I’ll just be trying to get through something routine, like opening a file or answering an email, and it’s like my body is moving through mud. And in meetings, someone will ask me something simple, something I should be able to answer right away, and there’s this… pause. Like I hear them, but it takes me a second to make myself respond.\n\nIt reminds me of when the copier jams and everyone stands there pressing the same button even though it clearly isn’t going to work. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes, I guess people have noticed I’m slower. I don’t think I’ve been pacing around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending time with friends and family since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Not always in the obvious way, like telling people “I’m not coming.” It’s more that I stop answering, or I let things slide until it’s too late to go.\n\nAt work, I’ve missed deadlines I normally would’ve been on top of, and I’ve avoided some difficult employee meetings—ones I used to handle all the time. I keep thinking I’ll get myself together enough to do it, and then the day just… moves past me.\n\nAnd the library volunteering, I haven’t been showing up for that. I used to like shelving returns because it was quiet and there was this one older man who always asked for mystery books but never remembered the authors’ names—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I just… I haven’t been going because I can’t keep myself steady enough to follow through. So yes, I think I’ve been pulling away.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline that you still find yourself looking forward to or participating in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of anything I really look forward to in the way I used to.\n\nI still do some things, I guess, but it feels more like because they’re on the calendar or because someone expects me to. Like I’ll have the thought, “Oh, I used to like that,” but the actual pull toward it isn’t really there. Even little things—stopping for coffee from that place near work, or browsing books at the library—somehow it all feels like too much effort, and then I end up just going home.\n\nSorry, I know I’m sort of circling around it. I think the answer is… no, not much comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find joy or interest in?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of one, and my mind kind of jumps around.\n\nLike, I used to be the person who had little routines—coffee, books, maybe texting someone back because something funny happened at work. There was this mug in the break room with a chip in it that everyone hated using, and for some reason I always thought it was funny when it ended up on my desk… Sorry, that’s not really an answer.\n\nI guess I don’t know if I’d call it joy. Sometimes something is distracting for a minute, or it’s familiar, but I don’t really feel that interest grabbing me the way it used to. I’m having a hard time naming anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're a bad person because of what happened with your father?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit.\n\nI keep replaying every decision I made about my dad’s appointments—whether I pushed hard enough, whether I missed something, whether I was too tired that day and it showed. And then I start thinking about the conversations with my relatives, the tense ones, where everyone had an opinion and I’d get defensive or shut down.\n\nIt all sort of piles up, and then it turns into this thought that I’m just… a selfish daughter. Like I didn’t do it right, or I didn’t care enough, even though I know I was trying. And then it spreads to work too, like I’m useless there, too, because I can’t keep up the way I used to.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad thing happens, I probably deserve it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things unrelated to your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t stay neatly about my dad, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt starts there, usually—like I’ll go over every appointment I took him to, every call I made or didn’t make, and then those arguments or tense talks with relatives, where I think maybe I was too sharp or too defensive. Like I hear them, but it takes me a second to make myself respond.\n\nIt reminds me of when the copier jams and everyone stands there pressing the same button even though it clearly isn’t going to work. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes, I guess people have noticed I’m slower. I don’t think I’ve been pacing around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending time with friends and family since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Not always in the obvious way, like telling people “I’m not coming.” It’s more that I stop answering, or I let things slide until it’s too late to go.\n\nAt work, I’ve missed deadlines I normally would’ve been on top of, and I’ve avoided some difficult employee meetings—ones I used to handle all the time. I keep thinking I’ll get myself together enough to do it, and then the day just… moves past me.\n\nAnd the library volunteering, I haven’t been showing up for that. I used to like shelving returns because it was quiet and there was this one older man who always asked for mystery books but never remembered the authors’ names—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I just… I haven’t been going because I can’t keep myself steady enough to follow through. So yes, I think I’ve been pulling away.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline that you still find yourself looking forward to or participating in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of anything I really look forward to in the way I used to.\n\nI still do some things, I guess, but it feels more like because they’re on the calendar or because someone expects me to. Like I’ll have the thought, “Oh, I used to like that,” but the actual pull toward it isn’t really there. Even little things—stopping for coffee from that place near work, or browsing books at the library—somehow it all feels like too much effort, and then I end up just going home.\n\nSorry, I know I’m sort of circling around it. I think the answer is… no, not much comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find joy or interest in?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of one, and my mind kind of jumps around.\n\nLike, I used to be the person who had little routines—coffee, books, maybe texting someone back because something funny happened at work. There was this mug in the break room with a chip in it that everyone hated using, and for some reason I always thought it was funny when it ended up on my desk… Sorry, that’s not really an answer.\n\nI guess I don’t know if I’d call it joy. Sometimes something is distracting for a minute, or it’s familiar, but I don’t really feel that interest grabbing me the way it used to. I’m having a hard time naming anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're a bad person because of what happened with your father?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit.\n\nI keep replaying every decision I made about my dad’s appointments—whether I pushed hard enough, whether I missed something, whether I was too tired that day and it showed. And then I start thinking about the conversations with my relatives, the tense ones, where everyone had an opinion and I’d get defensive or shut down.\n\nIt all sort of piles up, and then it turns into this thought that I’m just… a selfish daughter. Like I didn’t do it right, or I didn’t care enough, even though I know I was trying. And then it spreads to work too, like I’m useless there, too, because I can’t keep up the way I used to.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad thing happens, I probably deserve it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things unrelated to your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t stay neatly about my dad, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt starts there, usually—like I’ll go over every appointment I took him to, every call I made or didn’t make, and then those arguments or tense talks with relatives, where I think maybe I was too sharp or too defensive. I’ll be at work and make some small mistake, or even just not answer something fast enough, and suddenly it feels like proof that I’m useless there too.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as unrelated, because it all kind of connects in my head. Like I’m a selfish daughter, and then a bad employee, and then just… someone who deserves whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things you did or didn't do in your daily life, unrelated to your father's health or decline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling guilty about things unrelated to your father's health or decline?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about a year and a couple months. Maybe a year and two months, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt started around when my dad’s health and behavior started changing, and I had to step in more with caregiving. At first I thought it was just guilt about him, you know, but then it started attaching itself to other things too… work stuff, everyday things. I lose track of time a bit because that whole period feels like one long stretch, but… yeah, about a year and two months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty most of the time, even when you're not thinking about your father?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think. I used to tell employees at work not to talk to themselves that way, which is almost funny, except not really. But yes… it’s there a lot. Like I deserve whatever happens because I couldn’t get any of it right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I do. Even when nothing is really happening, it’s like my mind goes back over things anyway.

I’ll start thinking about every decision I made with my father’s appointments, whether I picked the wrong time or pushed too hard or not hard enough, and then I remember those awful tense conversations with relatives. And somehow it turns into this bigger thing, like… I’m a selfish daughter.

And then it follows me to work too, which doesn’t even make sense, because one minute I’m answering an email or looking at some HR form, and suddenly I’m thinking I’m useless there too. Like I deserve whatever happens because I couldn’t get any of it right.

Sorry, I know I’m circling around it. But yes, even without a specific reminder, that feeling is there.

---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came because I just... I can’t get this feeling out of my head that I’m being punished. Like some kind of judgment has already been made against me because of how my father declined, and I should have done something different.\n\nMy sister keeps showing me things, doctor’s notes and all that, saying I did everything right, that there wasn’t more I could’ve done. She’s always been the organized one, you know, folders and tabs and copies of everything. But even when I look at it, it doesn’t change anything inside. It still feels like I’m guilty somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling, like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nI’ll be sitting at my desk in HR, looking at forms or emails, and it’s like none of it can really get through to me. Or I’ll stare at the garden beds at home — I used to notice what needed trimming, what was coming up — and now it just feels far away, like it doesn’t reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about something specific that you did or didn't do regarding your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not one single thing, exactly. I keep replaying all of it — every appointment I scheduled or didn’t schedule fast enough, every phone call, every time I got short with my relatives because they were asking the same questions over and over.\n\nAnd then I’ll be at work, trying to answer some benefits question, and I think, how can I even help anyone when I couldn’t help my own father? It makes me feel like I’m selfish… like I was a bad daughter. And then I feel useless at work too, like I deserve whatever comes next.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your father declined?\n\nPatient: About… a year and two months, I think. It started when my father’s health and behavior began changing and I had to step in more, with caregiving and decisions and all of that.\n\nIt’s strange, because I can remember little details from then — like the folder I started keeping papers in, and my sister telling me I needed better dividers for it — but the time itself feels blurry. But yes… roughly fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really think about pleasure the way I used to, I guess.\n\nLike, I used to have routines, things I did after work or on weekends, but now when someone asks me what I enjoy, my mind kind of goes blank. It reminds me of when my coworker asked if I was taking any vacation time, and I started explaining our PTO rollover policy instead of answering her, which was… not what she meant.\n\nSo, I don’t know. I can’t really give you a clean answer. I haven’t been paying attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know. I haven’t been tracking it.\n\nI mean, my clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I think maybe they were just washed wrong or I’m wearing the older black pants from work, the ones with the waistband that never sat right even before all this. I used to keep receipts for returns in this little envelope in my desk, and now half the time I can’t even find the envelope, so weighing myself regularly has not really been something I’ve kept up with.\n\nSo… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a significant change. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I… not like a plan. I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut I do catch myself thinking, some mornings, that it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Especially when I already know there are going to be calls from the care agency, and then work problems waiting, and I’m trying to remember which voicemail was from who… It’s not that I’m setting out to do something. It’s more like… not wanting to have to start the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless and unable to sit still since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe more slow than restless.\n\nPeople at work have started asking if I’m sick, which is embarrassing because I don’t feel like I’m doing anything different on purpose. I’ll just be trying to get through something routine, like opening a file or answering an email, and it’s like my body is moving through mud. And in meetings, someone will ask me something simple, something I should be able to answer right away, and there’s this… pause. Like I hear them, but it takes me a second to make myself respond.\n\nIt reminds me of when the copier jams and everyone stands there pressing the same button even though it clearly isn’t going to work. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, yes, I guess people have noticed I’m slower. I don’t think I’ve been pacing around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending time with friends and family since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Not always in the obvious way, like telling people “I’m not coming.” It’s more that I stop answering, or I let things slide until it’s too late to go.\n\nAt work, I’ve missed deadlines I normally would’ve been on top of, and I’ve avoided some difficult employee meetings—ones I used to handle all the time. I keep thinking I’ll get myself together enough to do it, and then the day just… moves past me.\n\nAnd the library volunteering, I haven’t been showing up for that. I used to like shelving returns because it was quiet and there was this one older man who always asked for mystery books but never remembered the authors’ names—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I just… I haven’t been going because I can’t keep myself steady enough to follow through. So yes, I think I’ve been pulling away.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline that you still find yourself looking forward to or participating in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of anything I really look forward to in the way I used to.\n\nI still do some things, I guess, but it feels more like because they’re on the calendar or because someone expects me to. Like I’ll have the thought, “Oh, I used to like that,” but the actual pull toward it isn’t really there. Even little things—stopping for coffee from that place near work, or browsing books at the library—somehow it all feels like too much effort, and then I end up just going home.\n\nSorry, I know I’m sort of circling around it. I think the answer is… no, not much comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find joy or interest in?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of one, and my mind kind of jumps around.\n\nLike, I used to be the person who had little routines—coffee, books, maybe texting someone back because something funny happened at work. There was this mug in the break room with a chip in it that everyone hated using, and for some reason I always thought it was funny when it ended up on my desk… Sorry, that’s not really an answer.\n\nI guess I don’t know if I’d call it joy. Sometimes something is distracting for a minute, or it’s familiar, but I don’t really feel that interest grabbing me the way it used to. I’m having a hard time naming anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're a bad person because of what happened with your father?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit.\n\nI keep replaying every decision I made about my dad’s appointments—whether I pushed hard enough, whether I missed something, whether I was too tired that day and it showed. And then I start thinking about the conversations with my relatives, the tense ones, where everyone had an opinion and I’d get defensive or shut down.\n\nIt all sort of piles up, and then it turns into this thought that I’m just… a selfish daughter. Like I didn’t do it right, or I didn’t care enough, even though I know I was trying. And then it spreads to work too, like I’m useless there, too, because I can’t keep up the way I used to.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad thing happens, I probably deserve it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things unrelated to your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t stay neatly about my dad, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt starts there, usually—like I’ll go over every appointment I took him to, every call I made or didn’t make, and then those arguments or tense talks with relatives, where I think maybe I was too sharp or too defensive. But then it spreads. I’ll be at work and make some small mistake, or even just not answer something fast enough, and suddenly it feels like proof that I’m useless there too.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as unrelated, because it all kind of connects in my head. Like I’m a selfish daughter, and then a bad employee, and then just… someone who deserves whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things you did or didn't do in your daily life, unrelated to your father's health or decline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. I mean, I know I keep circling back to my dad, but it doesn’t stop there.\n\nLike at work, if I forget to follow up on something or I don’t answer an email quickly enough, I’ll think about it all day. And it turns into this bigger thing, like, “Of course you messed that up, you’re useless.” Even little daily things start feeling like evidence against me.\n\nAnd then my mind goes back to the appointments, the tense conversations with relatives, all of it… and I end up feeling like I was a selfish daughter and I’m a useless employee, and maybe I deserve whatever happens. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling guilty about things unrelated to your father's health or decline?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about a year and a couple months. Maybe a year and two months, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt started around when my dad’s health and behavior started changing, and I had to step in more with caregiving. At first I thought it was just guilt about him, you know, but then it started attaching itself to other things too… work stuff, everyday things. I lose track of time a bit because that whole period feels like one long stretch, but… yeah, about a year and two months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty most of the time, even when you're not thinking about your father?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think. Even when I’m not directly thinking about my father, it’s like the feeling is already there.\n\nI’ll be doing something normal, like at work, and then I remember some decision I made about his appointments, or one of those tense conversations with relatives, and it all comes back around. But then it doesn’t stay there. It turns into… I’m selfish, I’m a bad daughter, and somehow also a useless employee, like everything I do proves it.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic. I used to tell employees at work not to talk to themselves that way, which is almost funny, except not really. But yes… it’s there a lot. Like I deserve whatever happens because I couldn’t get any of it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a bad person most of the time, even when you're not thinking about your father or anything specific you've done?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not always in a clear, “I’m a bad person” sentence, but it’s there underneath.\n\nI’ll start with something specific, like going over every decision I made about my father’s appointments, or replaying tense conversations with my relatives, and then somehow it spreads. I end up feeling like I’m selfish as a daughter, and then I’ll be at work thinking I’m useless there too, which is ridiculous because HR is literally about helping people sort things out… though sometimes the paperwork alone could make anyone feel useless, honestly.\n\nBut, sorry, yes—the answer is yes. Even when I’m not focused on one specific thing, I feel like there’s something wrong with me, like I deserve whatever happens because I couldn’t get any of it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a bad person even when nothing specific is happening or reminding you of your past actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Even when nothing is really happening, it’s like my mind goes back over things anyway.\n\nI’ll start thinking about every decision I made with my father’s appointments, whether I picked the wrong time or pushed too hard or not hard enough, and then I remember those awful tense conversations with relatives. And somehow it turns into this bigger thing, like… I’m a selfish daughter.\n\nAnd then it follows me to work too, which doesn’t even make sense, because one minute I’m answering an email or looking at some HR form, and suddenly I’m thinking I’m useless there too. Like I deserve whatever happens because I couldn’t get any of it right.\n\nSorry, I know I’m circling around it. But yes, even without a specific reminder, that feeling is there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I keep thinking I’ll get myself together enough to do it, and then the day just… moves past me.\n\nAnd the library volunteering, I haven’t been showing up for that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it is about who I am, not just what I’ve done. Or—well, it starts with what I’ve done, I guess. I keep going over every decision about my father’s appointments, like whether I should’ve pushed harder or called sooner, and then I replay those tense conversations with relatives.

And then it stops being about one appointment or one argument and turns into… I’m a selfish daughter. Like that’s just what I am. And then somehow it bleeds into work too, like I’ll be sitting there with employee forms or some policy issue, and I think, “You’re useless here too.”

So yes. It feels like I deserve whatever happens, because I couldn’t get any of it right.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came because I just... I can’t get this feeling out of my head that I’m being punished. She’s always been the organized one, you know, folders and tabs and copies of everything. But even when I look at it, it doesn’t change anything inside. It still feels like I’m guilty somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling, like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nI’ll be sitting at my desk in HR, looking at forms or emails, and it’s like none of it can really get through to me. Or I’ll stare at the garden beds at home — I used to notice what needed trimming, what was coming up — and now it just feels far away, like it doesn’t reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about something specific that you did or didn't do regarding your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not one single thing, exactly. It makes me feel like I’m selfish… like I was a bad daughter. I’ll just be trying to get through something routine, like opening a file or answering an email, and it’s like my body is moving through mud.
